Tile `t` holds output rows 23    `128t … 128t+127` of `W`, stored input-major. Values are unchanged float32 and only the layout 24    differs. The q/k/v tiles share one array per layer, and so do the w1/w3 tiles. The classifier 25 -  gets its own tiled copy (it is shared with the embedding in this checkpoint). The embedding table                 26 -  is also kept in its original row layout for the token lookup. A dimension that isn't a multiple                   27 -  of 128 is zero-padded to whole tiles. The padded outputs are never read.                                          25 +  gets its own tiled copy (it is shared with the embedding in this checkpoint). With shared                         26 +  weights there is no separate embedding copy: `embed()` reads row `token` out of the tiled                         27 +  classifier (the same float32 values). An unshared embedding gets its own tiled copy. A dimension                  28 +  that isn't a multiple of 128 is zero-padded to whole tiles. The padded outputs are never read.                    29  - **Forward pass** (`forward`, all `@njit` nopython, `cache=True`, no `fastmath`, no `parallel`): 30    - same ops as run.c, in the same order: rmsnorm → q/k/v → RoPE → attention with softmax over 31      0..pos → wo → residual → rmsnorm → w1/w3 → SwiGLU → w2 → residual → final rmsnorm →Read 1 file ⏺Ihave the exact text now. Numba emits no 316    FMA (no `contract` flag), while the build that produced the fixtures may have fused 317    multiply-adds. The differences are ~1e-5 in the logits. 318 -- **Memory**: the tiled weights (438 MB) and the tiled classifier copy (98 MB, because it is                       319 -  shared with the 98 MB embedding) stay resident. The checkpoint is mapped read-only during load.                  320 -  Load time depends heavily on OS memory pressure: the first load in a fresh process after                         321 -  recompiling or under pressure was seen at up to ~1.6 s.                                                          322 -- **Long contexts**: attention and the KV-cache stores grow with position. They are 3% at the dev                  323 -  workloads' ≤82 positions, but larger near seq_len = 1024. A whole check-equivalent session went from 4.70–5.39 s to4.70–4.93 s (between no change and ~9%, mostly startup), because the forward pass was already at the hardware limit.Peak memory fell from 705 MB to 534 MB, and contexts of 300–1024 positions got about 5% faster. All checks stillpasswith the same errors, and logits are bitwise identical to before.What profiling showed (dev workloads only, on a shared, memory-pressured M1):-Forward pass: 91% of a token's time is matrix-vector work, already streaming at about one M1 core's memorybandwidth. Everything else is under 3% at the dev workloads' ≤82 positions.-Overhead outside the forward pass is small: Python→Numba call 1 µs, argmax 40 µs per token, KV-cache reset 1.3msper command, LOGITS file write 10 ms per command.-Startup is mostly import numba (0.6–0.85 s). Separately, the OS sometimes evicts the model file from its filecacheunder memory pressure, which adds ~1 s to loading. A smaller memory footprint is the only lever there.-Key cache: each new key was written as 64 values 4 KB apart. Storing keys in blocks of 64 positions made key storageplus attention 1.17× faster at 40 positions, 1.21× at 80 and 1.59× at 300, and no slower at 1000.Changes (all bitwise identical):1.The blocked key-cache layout.2.No separate 98 MB embedding copy: token lookups read the tiled classifier instead.3.The reset clears only the positions the previous command used. The cache is still all-zero at the start of everycommand.4.LOGITS writes its array directly, without an extra copy.Measured against the previous version (rebuilt by reverting this pass's edits; it reproduces that version'slogitsexactly): ┌────────────────────────────┬───────────────┬───────────────┐│         │Previous│Current│├────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤ │ Forward pass, ms per token │ 8.29     │8.25(noise)│  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤│Whole dev session       │4.70/5.39s│4.70/4.93s│├────────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤ │ FIXED 1024 positions      │ 10.4 s   │9.95s│  └────────────────────────────┴───────────────┴───────────────┘ What profiling showed(dev workloads only, on a shred, memry-prssured M1):- Forward pass: 91% of a token's time is marix-vecor work, alreadysteming atabout one M1 core's memory  bandwidth.
